A defective lateral can cause wastewater backup into the dwelling. Repair history may help establish whether the condition has been resolved.

Document the lateral condition, repair evidence, and underwriting disposition.

**220.AD** Refer any risk with drainage fixtures that repeatedly overflow, drain slowly, or emit sewage odor. Require investigation of the cause before binding.

Recurring fixture problems may indicate blockage, venting failure, or sewer line impairment. Surface symptoms may precede a more severe backup event.

Document the fixture involved, reported symptoms, corrective action, and file decision.

**220.AE** Evaluate crawlspaces for standing water, saturated soil, plumbing leakage, and damaged vapor barriers. Refer any risk with ongoing moisture or water accumulation.

Crawlspace moisture can affect structural components, insulation, plumbing, and indoor conditions. Persistent water may indicate drainage or plumbing failure.

Document the crawlspace condition, source of information, and underwriting action.

**220.AF** Refer any risk with a basement used for finished living area when prior water entry is reported. Obtain details of the cause, affected finishes, and corrective action.

Finished below-grade areas may sustain greater damage from seepage, backup, and plumbing discharge. Prior losses may indicate continuing vulnerability.

Document the basement use, water history, repairs, and referral outcome.

**220.AG** Evaluate foundation openings, window wells, exterior doors, and utility penetrations when water intrusion is reported. Refer unresolved defects that permit water entry.

Openings near grade may allow surface water into below-grade or interior areas. Repair adequacy affects the likelihood of recurrence.

Document the opening involved, reported repair, and underwriting disposition.

**220.AH** Refer any risk with a history of water entering through window wells or below-grade openings. Require correction of drainage, covers, seals, or related conditions as appropriate.

Window well water entry can damage finished areas and conceal structural moisture. Corrective measures should address the path of entry.

Document the reported entry point, corrective action, and supporting evidence.

**220.AI** Review plumbing system changes made during remodeling or addition work. Refer installations that lack reliable information regarding condition or completion.

Modified plumbing may introduce new joints, supply lines, drainage paths, or appliance connections. Incomplete work can increase leakage and discharge exposure.

Document the scope of plumbing work, completion status, and underwriting action.

**220.AJ** Refer any risk with an unoccupied dwelling where water service remains active and leak detection is unavailable. Evaluate the need for protective water management measures.

Undetected leaks can continue for extended periods in an unoccupied dwelling. Water management practices can reduce duration and severity.

Document occupancy status, water service status, protective measures, and referral result.

**220.AK** Evaluate smart leak detection devices only when reliable installation and active monitoring are established. Do not treat a device as a substitute for correcting known defects.

Detection devices may reduce the duration of certain water losses. They do not eliminate exposure from defective plumbing, drainage, or maintenance conditions.

Document the device type, reported monitoring arrangement, and any underwriting credit or action.

**220.AL** Refer any risk where the applicant cannot identify the cause of prior interior water damage. Require sufficient information to determine whether the source was plumbing, backup, seepage, or another condition.

Unknown cause prevents reliable assessment of recurrence potential. Underwriting action must be based on a supported exposure determination.

Document the unknown condition, information requested, and final disposition.

**220.AM** Evaluate contractor invoices, inspection reports, photographs, and repair records submitted for water-related conditions. Refer conflicting, incomplete, or unreliable evidence.

Supporting records may establish cause, repair scope, and completion status. Inconsistent information may indicate unresolved exposure.

Document the records reviewed, material findings, and any information that remains outstanding.

**220.AN** Refer any risk with concealed plumbing leakage suspected behind walls, ceilings, cabinets, or flooring. Do not accept the risk until the source and resulting damage are addressed.

Concealed leakage can continue without visible surface evidence. Hidden damage may affect structural materials and increase future loss potential.

Document the suspected location, reported investigation, repair status, and underwriting decision.

**220.AO** Evaluate pipe insulation and heating arrangements where plumbing is located in unheated or poorly protected areas. Refer inadequate protection when freeze exposure is apparent.

Pipes in vulnerable areas may freeze when temperatures decline or heat is interrupted. Protective measures may reduce the chance of rupture.

Document the plumbing location, protective measures reported, and underwriting action.

**220.AP** Refer any risk with plumbing located in an attic, exterior wall, garage, crawlspace, or similar vulnerable area when freeze or leak history is reported. Obtain corrective action details.

Water discharge from concealed or elevated plumbing may spread before detection. Vulnerable placement may also increase freeze exposure.

Document the plumbing location, loss history, repairs, and referral outcome.

**220.AQ** Evaluate pressure regulators, shutoff valves, and visible plumbing controls when water pressure issues are reported. Refer evidence of pressure-related leakage or repeated fixture failure.

Excessive or unstable pressure may strain supply lines, fittings, and appliances. Pressure conditions can contribute to recurring discharge losses.

Document the reported pressure issue, components affected, corrective action, and file disposition.

**220.AR** Refer any risk with repeated toilet overflow, drain overflow, or fixture backup. Obtain the cause and repair information before binding.

Repeated fixture overflow may indicate drainage obstruction, plumbing defect, or improper use. The cause determines whether the exposure remains acceptable.

Document the fixture involved, cause reported, repairs completed, and underwriting decision.

**220.AS** Evaluate water filtration, softening, and treatment equipment when connected to the plumbing system. Refer prior leakage, bypass failure, or improper installation.

Water treatment equipment may introduce hoses, fittings, drains, and connections that can fail. Installation quality affects leakage potential.

Document the equipment type, connection arrangement, loss history, and underwriting action.

**220.AT** Refer any risk with aquarium, waterbed, indoor fountain, or similar water-containing property that has caused prior damage. Evaluate the adequacy of placement and maintenance.

Non-plumbing water sources can create interior discharge exposure. Prior damage may indicate poor placement, inadequate containment, or maintenance failure.

Document the water source, prior damage, mitigation measures, and referral outcome.

**220.AU** Evaluate irrigation systems, exterior spigots, and hose connections when exterior leakage has affected the dwelling. Refer unresolved leakage near the foundation.

Exterior water sources can contribute to soil saturation and foundation moisture. Leakage may also indicate damaged piping or inadequate winterization.

Document the exterior water source, reported condition, repair information, and underwriting disposition.

**220.AV** Refer any risk with a history of exterior pipe rupture or irrigation leakage that caused interior or foundation damage. Obtain evidence that the cause has been corrected.

Exterior system failures may remain concealed until substantial water damage occurs. Repairs should address the failed component and related drainage condition.

Document the reported event, damaged area, repairs, and underwriting action.

**220.AW** Evaluate municipal water supply interruptions or pressure events when the applicant reports related water damage. Refer any risk with recurring damage tied to supply conditions.

Supply interruptions and pressure changes may affect plumbing components and backup conditions. Recurring events require review of mitigation and location-specific exposure.

Document the reported supply event, property impact, mitigation measures, and referral result.

**220.AX** Refer any risk with a history of floodwater, surface water, or groundwater entering the dwelling when the source cannot be separated from plumbing or backup damage. Obtain sufficient cause information before offering related coverage.

Water source classification affects underwriting treatment and coverage handling. Mixed causes may obscure the actual condition of the property.

Document the reported water source, information obtained, and final underwriting disposition.

**220.AY** Evaluate prior mitigation work involving drainage systems, foundation waterproofing, sump equipment, or plumbing replacement. Refer work that lacks completion information or appears temporary.

Mitigation may reduce exposure when properly completed and maintained. Temporary or undocumented work may not resolve the underlying condition.

Document the mitigation work, completion evidence, and underwriting action.

**220.AZ** Refer any risk with damaged drywall, insulation, flooring, cabinetry, or structural material following water loss when remediation status is unclear. Require evidence that affected materials have been addressed.

Water-damaged materials may conceal continuing moisture or deterioration. Restoration status is material to property condition evaluation.

Document the damaged materials, remediation information, and file decision.

**220.BA** Evaluate water loss conditions reported by an inspection, appraisal, claim file, or applicant statement. Refer material discrepancies among available sources.

Different sources may describe different causes, dates, locations, or repair status. Discrepancies must be resolved before acceptance.

Document the source information reviewed, discrepancy identified, and resolution obtained.

**220.BB** Refer any risk with evidence of unauthorized plumbing alterations, improvised connections, or exposed water lines. Require evaluation and correction by an appropriate party.

Unauthorized alterations may fail without warning and may not provide reliable shutoff or drainage control. Improvised work can increase leakage exposure.

Document the alteration observed or reported, corrective action, and underwriting outcome.

**220.BC** Evaluate vacant structures for drained plumbing systems, secured water service, and protection from water entry. Refer any risk with uncertain water management during vacancy.

Vacant structures are vulnerable to delayed discovery of leakage, freeze damage, and drainage failure. Protective measures must be credible and maintained.

Document the vacancy condition, water management arrangement, and referral disposition.

**220.BD** Refer any risk with a prior claim involving water damage to multiple areas of the dwelling. Obtain information showing whether the damage resulted from a single source or separate conditions.

Damage affecting multiple areas may indicate extensive migration or repeated water events. Cause determination is necessary to evaluate residual exposure.

Document the damaged areas, reported cause, repairs, and underwriting decision.

**220.BE** Evaluate attached garages, utility rooms, and mechanical rooms for plumbing and drainage exposure. Refer active leaks, floor drain backup, or water migration toward finished areas.

Service areas may contain water equipment that can discharge without prompt detection. Drainage conditions can affect adjacent living space.

Document the area evaluated, water equipment present, reported condition, and file action.

**220.BF** Refer any risk with a floor drain that has backed up, overflowed, or failed to drain. Obtain the cause and repair information before acceptance.

Floor drain problems may indicate obstruction, sewer backup, or drainage system failure. The condition may affect lower-level damage potential.

Document the drain condition, cause reported, repairs completed, and underwriting disposition.

**220.BG** Evaluate whether prior water repairs addressed all affected areas and related sources. Refer partial repairs or unresolved conditions identified during review.

Repair limited to visible damage may leave the source or concealed damage unaddressed. Complete remediation supports a reliable property condition assessment.

Document the repair scope, source correction, outstanding concerns, and underwriting action.

**220.BH** Refer any risk that does not provide sufficient information to evaluate material water exposure. Do not bind until the missing information is obtained or authorized underwriting direction is recorded.

Water exposure decisions require reliable information regarding cause, condition, and corrective action. Incomplete information may prevent an accurate eligibility determination.

Document the information requested, response received, and final underwriting disposition.

**220.BI** Record all water exposure decisions in a clear underwriting note. Include the condition reviewed, evidence considered, referral activity, and final action.

Consistent file documentation supports auditability and appropriate future servicing. Clear notes also assist claim review when a water event is later reported.

Document the completed underwriting note in the policy file.

## Rule 230 — Liability Hazards

**230.A** Refer any risk with a swimming pool unless the hazard controls are acceptable. Require a swimming pool barrier meeting the minimum height of 4 feet feet.

A swimming pool creates an attractive nuisance and increases premises liability exposure. Effective barriers reduce unauthorized access and support defensible underwriting.

Document the pool location, barrier condition, and access controls in underwriting notes. Retain hazard photographs when available.

**230.B** Refer any risk with a pool barrier showing damage, gaps, or unsecured access. Require correction of material barrier defects before accepting the risk.

Damaged barriers may permit unsupervised access to the pool area. Unsecured access increases the likelihood of severe bodily injury claims.

Document the observed defect and the corrective action required. Record confirmation of acceptable correction before binding.

**230.C** Refer any risk with a pool gate that does not close and latch securely. Require the gate to remain secured when the pool is not supervised.

Pool gates are a primary access point for guests and trespassers. A gate that remains open defeats the intended barrier protection.

Document the gate condition and the insured's stated corrective action. Retain inspection notes or photographs supporting the decision.

**230.D** Refer any risk with a diving feature, slide, or elevated pool access. Evaluate the feature for unsafe design, maintenance, or supervision concerns.

Diving and elevated access features increase the severity potential of pool injuries. Unsafe features may create a foreseeable premises liability condition.

Document the feature, its condition, and any underwriting action taken. Record the basis for acceptance, modification, or decline.

**230.E** Refer any risk with an unfenced natural pond used for swimming or recreation. Evaluate access, visibility, and warning conditions.

Natural water features can attract guests, children, and unauthorized visitors. Uncontrolled access may create significant liability exposure.

Document the water feature and its intended use. Record the hazard controls considered during underwriting review.

**230.F** Refer any risk with a hot tub, spa, or similar water feature that lacks adequate access control. Require correction of unsafe access conditions.

Hot tubs and spas present drowning, slip, and supervision hazards. Restricted access helps reduce accidental entry by guests and visitors.

Document the location, condition, and access controls for the water feature. Record any required corrective action in underwriting notes.

**230.G** Refer any risk with a trampoline. Evaluate anchoring, placement, condition, and access control before accepting the exposure.

Trampolines present frequent bodily injury allegations involving guests and neighborhood visitors. Poor maintenance or unrestricted use increases the hazard.

Document the trampoline condition and its location on the premises. Record the underwriting decision and any restrictions imposed.

**230.H** Refer any risk with a trampoline placed near structures, fencing, trees, or other hard surfaces. Require relocation or removal when the placement presents an unacceptable hazard.

Nearby surfaces can increase injury severity when a user falls from the trampoline. Improper placement may also create property damage exposure.

Document the placement concern and the corrective action required. Record confirmation that the hazard has been addressed.

**230.I** Refer any risk with playground equipment showing deterioration, instability, or exposed sharp surfaces. Require correction, removal, or replacement of unsafe equipment.

Playground equipment may cause serious injury when it is poorly maintained. Visible deterioration may indicate inadequate premises care.

Document the equipment condition and location. Record the corrective action required before acceptance.

**230.J** Refer any risk with elevated play equipment lacking appropriate safety features. Evaluate fall exposure and access by guests or visitors.

Elevated equipment creates a heightened bodily injury hazard. Unsafe access or landing conditions may increase claim severity.

Document the equipment type, condition, and surrounding surface. Record the underwriting disposition and supporting rationale.

**230.K** Refer any risk with a tree house, platform, or similar elevated structure used by children or guests. Require acceptable construction, access, and guard protection.

Elevated recreational structures can cause fall injuries and premises liability claims. Informal construction may not provide reliable protection.

Document the structure, its use, and the observed hazard controls. Record any required correction or underwriting restriction.

**230.L** Refer any risk with unsecured construction materials, tools, or debris. Require removal or secure storage of hazardous items.

Construction debris and unsecured tools create trip, cut, and injury hazards. They may also attract children and unauthorized visitors.

Document the condition and the area affected. Record the corrective action required for eligibility.

**230.M** Refer any risk undergoing substantial repair or renovation activity. Evaluate public access, worksite controls, and contractor involvement.

Active work areas can create hazards outside normal residential occupancy. Uncontrolled work conditions may increase bodily injury and property damage exposure.

Document the nature of the work and the visible safety controls. Record the basis for the underwriting decision.

**230.N** Refer any risk with an incomplete exterior surface, open excavation, or unprotected opening. Require the hazard to be secured before accepting the risk.

Openings and excavations create foreseeable injury hazards for occupants and visitors. Inadequate protection may support allegations of negligent maintenance.

Document the condition and the protective measures in place. Record any correction required before binding.

**230.O** Refer any risk with a damaged walkway, driveway, stairway, or porch surface. Require repair of conditions that create a material trip or fall hazard.

Uneven walking surfaces are a common source of premises liability claims. Visible defects may indicate a known and preventable hazard.

Document the location and condition of the surface. Record the required repair and confirmation of completion.

**230.P** Refer any risk with missing, loose, or damaged handrails. Require secure handrails where stairs or elevated walking areas present a fall hazard.

Handrails assist safe movement and reduce fall potential. Defective rails may fail when relied upon by a visitor.

Document the handrail condition and affected area. Record the corrective action required in underwriting notes.

**230.Q** Refer any risk with damaged decking, balconies, railings, or elevated porches. Evaluate structural condition and exposure to guests.

Elevated exterior structures can produce severe bodily injury when they fail. Deterioration may also indicate inadequate maintenance practices.

Document the observed condition and access to the structure. Record the underwriting action and supporting rationale.

**230.R** Refer any risk with an unsecured ladder accessible to children or visitors. Require secure storage or removal of the ladder when not in use.

Accessible ladders may allow unauthorized access to roofs, pools, and elevated structures. Improper storage increases premises liability exposure.

Document the ladder location and storage condition. Record the corrective action required before acceptance.

**230.S** Refer any risk with a roof accessible by exterior climbing features, ladders, or structures. Evaluate the potential for unauthorized access and injury.

Accessible roofs create fall hazards for guests and trespassers. The condition may also increase attractive nuisance concerns.

Document the access condition and any protective measures. Record the basis for the underwriting decision.

**230.T** Refer any risk with aggressive, threatening, or poorly controlled animals. Do not accept the risk without satisfactory evidence that the liability hazard is controlled.

Animal behavior can create severe bodily injury exposure. Prior aggressive conduct may indicate a foreseeable recurrence risk.

Document the animal type, reported behavior, and control measures. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.U** Refer any risk with an animal that has caused injury, threatened a person, or damaged property. Evaluate the circumstances and the adequacy of current controls.

Prior incidents may indicate an increased likelihood of future liability claims. Continued exposure may be unacceptable when controls are inadequate.

Document the reported incident and the current control measures. Record the reason for acceptance, restriction, or decline.

**230.V** Refer any risk with animals roaming beyond the insured premises. Require confinement adequate to prevent uncontrolled contact with the public.

Animals that roam may injure persons or damage neighboring property. Lack of confinement can support allegations of negligent control.

Document the confinement method and the animal's access to public areas. Record required corrective action before binding.

**230.W** Refer any risk with livestock, farm animals, or similar animal exposure. Evaluate fencing, handling practices, and public access.

Large animals can cause serious injury and substantial property damage. Their presence may exceed the intended personal liability hazard profile.

Document the animal exposure and premises controls. Record the underwriting decision and rationale.

**230.X** Refer any risk with a dog maintained for security or protection purposes. Evaluate restraint, warning conditions, and access by visitors.

Security animals may present heightened bite and attack exposure. Visitors may encounter the animal without adequate warning or control.

Document the animal's purpose and restraint method. Record the hazard controls supporting the decision.

**230.Y** Refer any risk with a firearm range, target practice area, or discharge activity on the premises. Evaluate access controls and proximity to neighboring property.

Firearm activity presents severe bodily injury and property damage potential. Informal recreational use may create uncontrolled liability exposure.

Document the activity, location, and safety controls described. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.Z** Refer any risk with explosives, fireworks, pyrotechnic materials, or similar hazardous items. Do not accept ongoing hazardous activity without underwriting approval.

Explosive materials can cause catastrophic bodily injury and property damage. Storage or use may create exposure beyond normal residential operations.

Document the hazardous material or activity identified. Record the referral outcome and any required action.

**230.AA** Refer any risk with a business operation conducted from the residence. Evaluate customer visits, deliveries, equipment, and public access.

Business activity may create liability exposure not contemplated by ordinary personal occupancy. Customer traffic and operational hazards may materially change the risk.

Document the business activity and the associated premises exposure. Record the underwriting decision and supporting rationale.

**230.AB** Refer any risk with clients, customers, patients, students, or other visitors attending the premises. Evaluate visitor access and supervision conditions.

Regular visitor traffic increases the likelihood of premises liability allegations. Business-related visitors may also encounter hazards not present in ordinary residential use.

Document the visitor activity and areas accessible to visitors. Record the underwriting action taken.

**230.AC** Refer any risk with daycare, child care, tutoring, or similar supervision activity. Refer the exposure for review before acceptance.

Supervision of children creates heightened bodily injury and care-related liability exposure. The presence of unrelated children may materially increase hazard frequency.

Document the activity and the areas used for supervision. Record the referral decision and rationale.

**230.AD** Refer any risk with elder care, assisted care, or similar personal care activity. Evaluate visitor safety, supervision, and accessibility hazards.

Care activities can create allegations involving falls, supervision, and negligent care. Vulnerable visitors may experience greater injury severity.

Document the care activity and the associated premises conditions. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.AE** Refer any risk with rental occupancy or guest lodging activity. Evaluate turnover, visitor access, and property condition.

Rental occupants and transient guests may be unfamiliar with the premises hazards. Frequent occupancy changes can reduce the insured's ability to monitor conditions.

Document the occupancy activity and the areas available to occupants. Record the underwriting action and rationale.

**230.AF** Evaluate the rental day allowance when rental use is disclosed. Refer the risk if the disclosed use presents a liability hazard outside the intended occupancy profile.

Rental activity can increase visitor traffic and reduce direct control over premises conditions. Liability exposure may arise from guest injuries or damage to neighboring property.

Document the rental use and the underwriting evaluation. Record the basis for the decision.

**230.AG** Refer any risk with a vacant or unoccupied dwelling. Evaluate access control, maintenance, and conditions that may attract unauthorized entry.

Vacant property can develop unnoticed hazards and may attract trespassers. Delayed maintenance can increase premises liability exposure.

Document the occupancy condition and visible hazard controls. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.AH** Refer any risk with unsecured doors, windows, gates, or other access points. Require reasonable measures to limit unauthorized entry.

Unauthorized visitors may encounter unsafe conditions without the insured's knowledge. Poor access control increases trespass and premises liability concerns.

Document the access condition and corrective action required. Record confirmation of acceptable protection.

**230.AI** Refer any risk with a nuisance condition affecting neighboring property. Evaluate the source, duration, and potential for bodily injury or property damage.

Nuisance conditions can produce liability allegations from neighbors and visitors. Ongoing conditions may indicate inadequate maintenance or supervision.

Document the condition and the reported impact on surrounding property. Record the underwriting decision and supporting rationale.

**230.AJ** Refer any risk with excessive debris, refuse, or unsanitary exterior conditions. Require cleanup where the condition creates a liability hazard.

Accumulated debris may cause injuries, attract pests, or obstruct safe access. Unsanitary conditions may also indicate broader maintenance concerns.

Document the affected area and visible hazard condition. Record the corrective action required.

**230.AK** Refer any risk with a known sinkhole, unstable ground, retaining wall concern, or earth movement hazard. Evaluate access restrictions and visible maintenance conditions.

Unstable ground can cause sudden injury and property damage. Inadequate barriers may expose guests and visitors to foreseeable danger.

Document the hazard location and protective measures. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.AL** Refer any risk with a retaining wall showing movement, cracking, leaning, or deterioration. Require evaluation of any condition that may create collapse or fall exposure.

Failing retaining walls can injure persons and damage adjoining property. Visible deterioration may indicate an active maintenance issue.

Document the observed condition and affected area. Record the corrective action or referral outcome.

**230.AM** Refer any risk with a private bridge, dock, pier, or similar structure. Evaluate condition, access, guard protection, and public exposure.

Waterfront and elevated structures create fall, collapse, and drowning hazards. Deteriorated construction may increase claim severity.

Document the structure, its use, and the observed condition. Record the underwriting decision and rationale.

**230.AN** Refer any risk with a dock or pier used by guests or visitors. Require reasonable maintenance and controlled access to hazardous areas.

Guest use increases the likelihood of injuries from falls, slips, or water access. Lack of maintenance may support allegations of negligent premises care.

Document guest access and the hazard controls in place. Record any corrective action required.

**230.AO** Refer any risk with a boat launch, private water access, or shoreline recreation area. Evaluate access control, surface condition, and foreseeable visitor use.

Water access areas can create drowning, slip, and injury exposure. Informal use by guests or neighbors may be difficult to supervise.

Document the water access feature and its condition. Record the underwriting evaluation and disposition.

**230.AP** Evaluate the outboard horsepower exclusion threshold when watercraft exposure is disclosed. Refer any watercraft use that presents a liability hazard beyond the intended personal risk profile.

Watercraft activity can create severe bodily injury and property damage exposure. The hazard may increase when operation, storage, or guest use is not controlled.

Document the disclosed watercraft exposure and its use. Record the underwriting decision and rationale.

**230.AQ** Refer any risk with recreational vehicles operated from the premises or stored with unrestricted access. Evaluate operation areas, supervision, and access by guests.

Recreational vehicle use can create bodily injury and property damage claims. Uncontrolled access may allow inexperienced users to operate hazardous equipment.

Document the vehicle exposure and storage conditions. Record the underwriting action taken.

**230.AR** Refer any risk with off-road vehicle trails, jumps, ramps, or recreational riding areas. Evaluate the potential for guest injury and neighboring property damage.

Recreational riding activity may create high-severity bodily injury exposure. Terrain features can increase the likelihood of loss.

Document the riding activity and location. Record the basis for acceptance, restriction, or decline.

**230.AS** Refer any risk with a garage, workshop, or outbuilding used for hazardous repair activity. Evaluate ventilation, storage, visitor access, and ignition sources.

Repair activities may involve tools, fuels, chemicals, and unsafe equipment. Uncontrolled work areas can create liability exposure for guests and visitors.

Document the activity and hazardous conditions observed. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.AT** Refer any risk with hazardous chemicals, fuel containers, or flammable materials stored in accessible areas. Require secure storage and reasonable separation from visitor areas.

Accessible hazardous materials can cause burns, poisoning, fires, and environmental damage. Improper storage may increase both injury and property damage potential.

Document the material type and storage condition. Record the corrective action required.

**230.AU** Refer any risk with a wood stove, fire pit, outdoor fireplace, or open flame area accessible to guests. Evaluate condition, placement, and supervision controls.

Open flame features can cause burns, smoke damage, and fire-related injuries. Guest use may increase exposure when controls are informal.

Document the feature and the observed safety conditions. Record the underwriting action and rationale.

**230.AV** Refer any risk with an unfenced fire pit or burn area near public access. Require adequate control of the area when the feature presents an injury hazard.

Burn areas may attract children, guests, and unauthorized visitors. Residual heat and ash can remain hazardous after active use ends.

Document the feature location and access controls. Record any corrective action required.

**230.AW** Refer any risk with a history of liability claims, complaints, or reported injuries arising from the premises. Evaluate whether the underlying hazard has been corrected.

Prior liability events may reveal recurring conditions or inadequate controls. Failure to correct the cause may increase the likelihood of another claim.

Document the reported event and the corrective measures taken. Record the underwriting rationale for the disposition.

**230.AX** Refer any risk with neighbor disputes involving property access, animals, noise, water runoff, or alleged damage. Evaluate whether an active liability hazard remains.

Neighbor disputes may indicate conditions likely to produce future liability allegations. Ongoing conflict can also complicate claim handling and risk assessment.

Document the nature of the dispute and the conditions described. Record the underwriting decision and rationale.

**230.AY** Evaluate the good neighbor coverage limit when neighbor-related liability exposure is disclosed. Refer any risk where the surrounding conditions indicate an unresolved liability hazard.

Neighbor-related exposures may arise from shared boundaries, access, drainage, and property use. Clear identification of the hazard supports consistent underwriting treatment.

Document the neighbor-related condition and the underwriting evaluation. Record the basis for the decision.

**230.AZ** Refer any risk with shared driveways, private roads, common walkways, or similar shared access areas. Evaluate maintenance responsibility and public use.

Shared access areas may create liability disputes when injury or damage occurs. Unclear maintenance responsibility can increase claim complexity.

Document the shared area and the known responsibility arrangement. Record the underwriting disposition and rationale.

**230.BA** Refer any risk with poor exterior lighting at entrances, walkways, stairs, or parking areas used by visitors. Require correction where inadequate lighting creates a material safety hazard.

Poor lighting can contribute to falls, security incidents, and unsafe navigation. Visitors may be unable to identify surface defects or elevation changes.

Document the lighting condition and affected area. Record the corrective action required before acceptance.

**230.BB** Refer any risk with a driveway, parking area, or vehicle access route that creates an unsafe condition for visitors. Evaluate visibility, surface condition, and separation from pedestrian areas.

Vehicle movement on residential premises can cause bodily injury and property damage. Poor visibility or uncontrolled access may increase the likelihood of accidents.

Document the access route and identified hazard. Record the underwriting action and rationale.

**230.BC** Refer any risk with a fence, gate, or wall that may collapse, obstruct access, or injure visitors. Require repair of material defects before accepting the risk.

Defective perimeter structures may create injury and property damage exposure. Their condition may also affect containment of animals and recreational hazards.

Document the structure condition and location. Record the required correction and confirmation of completion.

**230.BD** Refer any risk with unsecured abandoned property, discarded equipment, or hazardous personal property accessible to visitors. Require removal, secure storage, or other acceptable hazard control.

Discarded property can create trip, cut, entrapment, and injury hazards. Accessible items may attract children and unauthorized visitors.

Document the hazardous property and its location. Record the corrective action required.

**230.BE** Refer any risk where the insured cannot describe who controls access to the premises. Obtain sufficient information to determine whether the liability hazard is acceptable.

Unclear control of the premises can prevent effective hazard management. Liability may arise when visitors enter areas not actively supervised.

Document the access arrangement and responsible party information. Record the underwriting decision and rationale.

**230.BF** Refer any risk with a liability hazard that cannot be adequately evaluated from available information. Do not bind the risk until the hazard, controls, and responsible party are understood.

Incomplete information prevents reliable assessment of foreseeable injury and property damage exposure. A clear record supports consistent underwriting and defensible file handling.

Document the information gap and the action required to resolve it. Record the final underwriting disposition in the file.

## Rule 240 — Prior Loss History

**240.A** Obtain prior loss history before accepting, renewing, or changing a risk. Use carrier-approved loss sources and applicant disclosures.

Prior loss history identifies recurring hazards and unresolved exposure conditions. Treat missing history as an underwriting concern.

Document the source reviewed and the date of review. Record any unavailable history and the action taken.

**240.B** Compare applicant disclosures with available loss history. Refer material discrepancies for underwriting review.

Undisclosed losses may indicate incomplete risk information. Accurate history supports consistent classification.

Document the disclosed history and the verified history. Describe any discrepancy without editorial comment.

**240.C** Review reported, paid, denied, and open property claims occurring during the prior 3 years years. Include claims involving the applicant, household members, and insured location where available.

Relevant claim activity may reveal conditions that remain at the risk. Open claims may also affect valuation and eligibility.

Document the history reviewed and identify claim status. Retain loss details supporting the underwriting action.

**240.D** Refer any risk with 2 paid property claims in the applicable loss history. Do not bind the risk without underwriting authority.

Paid property claims may indicate recurring maintenance, occupancy, or exposure concerns. Referral permits review of cause, remediation, and trend.

Document the paid property claims prompting referral. Record the underwriting disposition and any conditions imposed.

**240.E** Identify the cause of every prior property claim. Classify the cause using carrier loss coding where available.

Loss cause is more relevant than claim count alone. Similar causes may indicate an unresolved condition.

Document the reported cause and the source of that information. Note when the cause cannot be confirmed.

**240.F** Distinguish weather-related claims from maintenance-related claims. Refer claims with unclear or conflicting cause information.

Weather losses may be isolated events, while maintenance losses may remain ongoing. Clear classification supports appropriate underwriting treatment.

Document the evidence used to classify the claim. Record the reason for any referral.

**240.G** Review prior water losses for source, location, and repair status. Refer repeated water-related loss activity.

Water losses may indicate plumbing defects, drainage issues, roof conditions, or seepage. Repairs must address the cause rather than visible damage alone.

Document the source of water and the affected area. Record repair information obtained from the applicant or inspection source.

**240.H** Refer any prior water loss involving seepage, leakage, backup, overflow, or drainage failure when remediation is unclear. Require satisfactory evidence of completed repairs before approval.

Unverified water repairs may leave the risk susceptible to recurring damage. Visible restoration does not establish correction of the underlying condition.

Document the claimed repair and supporting evidence. Record any underwriting requirement communicated to the producer.

**240.I** Review prior fire losses for origin, extent of damage, and repair status. Refer fire losses involving electrical, heating, cooking, or intentional-act concerns.

Fire losses may arise from hazards that remain after rebuilding. Origin information is necessary to evaluate corrective action.

Document the reported origin and repair status. Retain available fire reports, inspection findings, or applicant statements.

**240.J** Refer prior fire losses with unresolved origin or questionable remediation. Do not rely solely on a statement that repairs were completed.

Unresolved fire causation may indicate continuing hazard. Underwriting must confirm that the exposure is acceptable.

Document the unresolved issue and the evidence requested. Record the final underwriting decision.

**240.K** Review prior theft and vandalism losses for occupancy, security, and neighborhood conditions. Refer claims suggesting vacancy, unprotected property, or recurring criminal activity.

Theft and vandalism may reflect a condition that continues at the insured location. Security controls and occupancy information affect risk evaluation.

Document the reported circumstances and protective measures. Record any conditions required for approval.

**240.L** Verify whether prior theft losses involved forced entry, interior theft, or unexplained disappearance. Refer unexplained loss circumstances for further review.

Claim circumstances may affect credibility and future loss potential. Clear facts support consistent underwriting action.

Document the reported method of loss and available supporting information. Note any facts that remain unverified.

**240.M** Review prior wind, hail, and storm losses for roof condition and repair quality. Refer risks with evidence of unrepaired exterior damage.

Storm damage may expose existing construction defects or deferred maintenance. Incomplete repairs can increase future loss severity.

Document the damaged components and repair status. Retain inspection findings or applicant-provided evidence.

**240.N** Refer prior roof losses when the roof condition, age, material, or repair status is unclear. Require acceptable confirmation before approval where roof condition affects eligibility.

Roof losses may recur when damage remains uncorrected. Underwriting must evaluate the present condition rather than the reported claim outcome.

Document the information reviewed and any missing details. Record the basis for the underwriting action.

**240.O** Review prior liability claims for allegation, injury severity, location, and disposition. Refer claims involving hazardous conditions, animal incidents, or personal conduct concerns.

Liability claims may identify conditions that remain at the premises or within the household. The allegation may be relevant even when no payment occurred.

Document the allegation and claim disposition. Record facts supporting acceptance, restriction, or referral.

**240.P** Refer prior animal-related liability claims for underwriting review. Verify current animal ownership and any corrective action.

Animal losses may indicate continued bite, aggression, or control concerns. Current exposure information is required for a sound decision.

Document the reported animal involvement and current ownership status. Record any underwriting restriction or condition.

**240.Q** Review prior premises liability claims for the condition that caused the allegation. Verify whether the condition was repaired or removed.

Premises hazards may continue after claim closure. A closed claim does not establish that the hazard no longer exists.

Document the reported condition and corrective action. Retain supporting inspection information when available.

**240.R** Refer prior injury claims involving stairs, walkways, decks, pools, playground equipment, or similar premises features. Obtain current condition information before approval.

These features can create recurring liability exposure when maintenance is inadequate. Current condition information supports proper risk selection.

Document the feature involved and the condition reported. Record the evidence used to confirm remediation.

**240.S** Review prior claims involving mold, fungi, bacteria, rot, or moisture conditions. Refer risks with unresolved moisture source or incomplete remediation.

Moisture-related damage may continue when the source remains active. The fungi and bacteria aggregate does not determine underwriting eligibility.

Document the moisture source and remediation information. Record any exclusion, restriction, or referral action.

**240.T** Distinguish remediation work from cosmetic restoration. Require evidence that the underlying loss cause was addressed.

Cosmetic repairs may conceal conditions that remain capable of causing loss. Underwriting must evaluate corrective work for adequacy.

Document the remediation represented by the applicant. Record the evidence reviewed and any remaining concern.

**240.U** Review prior claims involving foundation movement, earth movement, settling, cracking, or structural damage. Refer risks with ongoing movement or unverified structural repairs.

Structural conditions may worsen over time and may not be resolved by surface repairs. Expert evaluation may be needed when the cause is uncertain.

Document the reported damage and repair status. Retain engineering, contractor, or inspection information when available.

**240.V** Refer prior collapse claims for underwriting review. Verify the cause, damaged property, and completed corrective action.

Collapse-related losses may reflect structural, maintenance, or environmental concerns. The loss cause affects future exposure evaluation.

Document the claim facts and repair evidence. Record the underwriting authority approving any exception.

**240.W** Review prior claims involving trees, branches, fences, sheds, or detached structures. Verify that damaged property was repaired and hazardous conditions were addressed.

Detached property losses may reveal maintenance concerns or unmanaged vegetation. Hazard conditions can remain after claim payment.

Document the damaged property and reported corrective action. Record any inspection finding affecting eligibility.

**240.X** Refer prior tree-related losses when hazardous vegetation remains near insured structures. Require satisfactory mitigation before approval where warranted.

Unmanaged vegetation may create recurring wind, impact, or access exposure. Mitigation reduces the potential for repeat damage.

Document the location of the vegetation and mitigation status. Retain photographs or inspection findings when available.

**240.Y** Review prior claims involving vacant, seasonal, rented, or unoccupied property. Verify current occupancy and property supervision.

Occupancy changes can materially affect loss frequency and severity. Unsupervised property may experience delayed discovery of damage.

Document the occupancy represented and the supporting source. Record any underwriting restriction based on occupancy.

**240.Z** Refer prior claims occurring during vacancy or unoccupancy. Obtain facts regarding supervision, utilities, maintenance, and repair completion.

Losses during reduced occupancy may indicate conditions that were not promptly identified. Current controls may affect acceptability.

Document the prior occupancy condition and current controls. Record the rationale for the underwriting decision.

**240.AA** Review prior losses involving business activity, home-based work, storage, or commercial use. Refer any loss suggesting unreported business exposure.

Business activity may create property or liability hazards outside personal lines appetite. Claim facts may reveal use not disclosed on the application.

Document the activity identified and the source of information. Record any required correction to the risk description.

**240.AB** Verify whether prior losses involved short-term rental, hosted occupancy, or compensated guest use. Refer risks with loss activity tied to rental exposure.

Rental activity may change occupancy, maintenance, and liability exposure. Accurate use classification is necessary before approval.

Document the use reported and the claim connection. Record the underwriting action taken.

**240.AC** Review prior claims involving renovations, construction, or major repairs. Verify whether work is complete and whether permits or inspections were obtained where applicable.

Construction-related losses may arise from temporary hazards or defective work. Incomplete work may continue to affect the risk.

Document the work described and current completion status. Record any available permit, inspection, or contractor information.

**240.AD** Refer prior losses involving contractor negligence, defective workmanship, or construction defects. Evaluate whether the condition remains at the insured location.

Defective work may create repeated water, fire, structural, or liability exposure. Claim closure does not establish that defective work was corrected.

Document the alleged defect and repair information. Record any referral findings and underwriting requirements.

**240.AE** Review prior equipment breakdown, electrical surge, appliance, and mechanical failure claims. Verify the condition of affected systems after repair or replacement.

Equipment losses may indicate aging systems, improper installation, or electrical concerns. Current system condition informs risk selection.

Document the affected equipment and corrective action. Record any unresolved system concern.

**240.AF** Refer prior electrical losses involving panel, wiring, breaker, outlet, or service equipment concerns. Require acceptable evidence of correction before approval.

Electrical defects may contribute to fire and equipment damage. Qualified repair information is needed when electrical hazards are alleged.

Document the electrical condition and repair source. Retain inspection or contractor evidence when available.

**240.AG** Review prior claims involving heating equipment, chimneys, fireplaces, stoves, or fuel systems. Verify that the involved equipment is currently safe and serviceable.

Heating-related claims may involve fire, smoke, carbon monoxide, or water damage exposure. Prior repairs may not address improper operation or installation.

Document the equipment involved and the reported corrective action. Record any inspection or service information obtained.

**240.AH** Refer prior smoke, soot, or combustion-related losses with unclear origin. Obtain sufficient information to determine whether the source remains present.

Combustion losses may arise from ongoing equipment or maintenance concerns. Underwriting requires a reliable explanation of the event.

Document the claimed origin and the facts reviewed. Record any unresolved concern requiring referral.

**240.AI** Review prior claims involving pools, spas, ponds, trampolines, or recreational features. Verify current condition, access controls, and use.

Recreational features may create continuing property and liability exposure. Claim history may reveal inadequate maintenance or supervision.

Document the feature involved and current protective measures. Record any required underwriting action.

**240.AJ** Refer prior pool or spa claims involving injury, drainage, leakage, or equipment malfunction. Evaluate whether corrective action addresses the cause of loss.

Pool and spa hazards may remain after repairs are completed. Current safeguards affect future exposure.

Document the reported cause and corrective action. Retain available inspection findings or applicant statements.

**240.AK** Review prior claims involving underground utilities, service lines, wells, septic systems, or drainage systems. Verify current operating condition and repair status.

Underground system losses may recur when the system remains damaged or poorly maintained. Surface conditions may not reveal the full exposure.

Document the affected system and the repair represented. Record any available service records or inspection findings.

**240.AL** Refer prior claims involving sewer backup, sump failure, drainage failure, or septic overflow when the cause is unresolved. Require adequate corrective information before approval.

These losses may recur due to site conditions, maintenance, or system capacity. A claim payment does not establish that future loss potential is controlled.

Document the source of loss and remediation status. Record the basis for accepting or declining the risk.

**240.AM** Review prior losses for evidence of fraud indicators, inconsistent statements, suspicious timing, or unusual circumstances. Refer concerns without accusing the applicant of misconduct.

Fraud indicators require careful handling and objective documentation. Underwriting must rely on verified facts and established authority.

Document the facts creating concern and the source of information. Record the referral without speculative conclusions.

**240.AN** Do not alter, omit, or minimize loss information to achieve eligibility. Escalate any request to disregard material claim history.

Accurate loss history is necessary for sound underwriting and regulatory compliance. Improper handling may distort risk selection.

Document the request and the escalation action. Record the final direction received from underwriting authority.

**240.AO** Review claim payment information separately from claim allegation information. Do not treat a closed claim without payment as irrelevant.

Claims without payment may still identify hazardous conditions or loss potential. The reported facts may be material to underwriting.

Document the disposition and underlying allegation. Record the relevance assigned to the claim.

**240.AP** Consider subrogation, recovery, salvage, and reimbursement information when evaluating prior loss severity. Do not treat recovery activity as evidence that the loss cause was corrected.

Financial recovery may reduce claim cost without eliminating exposure. Underwriting must evaluate physical conditions and current controls.

Document recovery information when available. Record the facts supporting the exposure assessment.

**240.AQ** Evaluate prior catastrophe losses based on present property condition and mitigation status. Do not assume catastrophe classification resolves maintenance concerns.

Catastrophe events may reveal vulnerabilities that remain after the event. Current repairs and protective measures determine ongoing exposure.

Document the catastrophe cause and current condition. Record mitigation information used in the decision.

**240.AR** Refer any prior loss that exceeds the large loss referral threshold. Obtain sufficient facts regarding cause, repair, and current exposure.

Large losses may indicate severe damage, unusual hazards, or inadequate remediation. Referral provides appropriate authority review.

Document the loss circumstances and current condition. Record the authority decision and any imposed conditions.

**240.AS** Review prior loss history for patterns involving the same area of property. Refer recurring damage affecting the same building component or premises feature.

Recurring damage may indicate that prior repairs addressed symptoms rather than cause. Pattern recognition supports consistent risk selection.

Document the area affected and the claim pattern observed. Record the evidence supporting any referral.

**240.AT** Review prior loss history for patterns involving the same cause of loss. Refer recurring causes even when claim amounts or locations differ.

Repeated cause patterns may indicate household practices, maintenance deficiencies, or uncorrected hazards. Cause-based review supports a fuller exposure assessment.

Document the cause pattern and relevant claim details. Record the underwriting conclusion.

**240.AU** Verify repairs through reliable evidence when prior loss history indicates material damage. Accept photographs, inspection findings, contractor records, or comparable evidence as appropriate.

Applicant statements may be incomplete or may not establish repair quality. Reliable evidence supports defensible underwriting decisions.

Document the evidence reviewed and its source. Record any limitation affecting reliance on the evidence.

**240.AV** Do not require repair evidence that is unrelated to the prior loss concern. Tailor underwriting requirements to the identified hazard.

Targeted requirements improve consistency and avoid unnecessary burden. The requirement must address a material exposure issue.

Document the hazard identified and the evidence requested. Record the reason the requirement was necessary.

**240.AW** Evaluate loss history with current inspection findings and application information. Refer material conflicts between current condition and reported repairs.

Current observations may reveal conditions not apparent from claim records. Conflicting information requires resolution before approval.

Document the conflicting facts and the source of each fact. Record the referral outcome.

**240.AX** Do not assume that prior loss history alone establishes ineligibility. Apply underwriting judgment to cause, recurrence, repair, and current condition.

Claim history must be evaluated in context. Consistent decisions require consideration of relevant risk characteristics.

Document the factors considered in the decision. Record any authority approval for an exception.

**240.AY** Apply the same loss history review standard to new business, renewal business, and material changes. Refer risks when new information changes the prior assessment.

Loss information may emerge after initial review or may change the risk profile. Ongoing review supports accurate underwriting.

Document the new information and its source. Record any resulting underwriting action.

**240.AZ** Obtain updated loss history when prior information is stale, incomplete, or inconsistent with current disclosures. Do not rely on unsupported assumptions about claim activity.

Incomplete information may conceal material changes in exposure. Updated review supports reliable risk selection.

Document the reason updated history was needed. Record the source and result of the updated review.

**240.BA** Record only factual loss information in the underwriting file. Avoid unsupported opinions regarding claimant conduct, intent, or credibility.

Objective documentation supports fair and consistent underwriting. Speculation may create avoidable compliance concerns.

Document the claim facts, source, and underwriting action. Use neutral language throughout the file.

**240.BB** Retain loss history documentation supporting acceptance, referral, restriction, or declination. Ensure the file shows the basis for the final underwriting decision.

Complete documentation supports audit review and consistent future handling. The record must connect claim facts to the action taken.

Document the final disposition and approving authority. Preserve supporting claim information in the underwriting file.

## Rule 250 — Occupancy, Vacancy and Rental

**250.A** Verify the stated occupancy before binding or continuing the risk. Refer any occupancy that differs from the application.

Accurate occupancy supports proper eligibility and rating. Unstated occupancy can conceal material exposure.

Document the occupancy statement in the file. Retain supporting contact notes.

**250.B** Confirm that the applicant has an insurable interest in the dwelling. Refer any ownership arrangement that is unclear or disputed.

Ownership affects authority to insure and maintain the premises. Unresolved ownership can impair claim handling.

Document the ownership interest described by the applicant. Retain available ownership evidence.

**250.C** Confirm whether a named insured resides at the premises. Refer any risk where residence is not clearly established.

Resident occupancy generally presents a different exposure than nonresident ownership. Clear residence status supports accurate classification.

Document the residence status of all named insureds. Record the source of the information.

**250.D** Refer any dwelling used as a seasonal or intermittent residence. Do not classify such use as continuous resident occupancy.

Intermittent use can delay discovery of damage or theft. The exposure may require underwriting review.

Document the stated use pattern in the file. Record available evidence of regular occupancy.

**250.E** Refer any nonprincipal residence where the insured does not regularly occupy the premises. Confirm who has responsibility for care and maintenance.

Nonprincipal residences may remain unattended for extended intervals. Maintenance responsibility affects loss potential.

Document the responsible party and contact information. Record the stated maintenance arrangement.

**250.F** Apply the vacancy condition when the dwelling lacks the customary presence of occupants and personal property. Treat vandalism as excluded after 60 days of vacancy.

Vacant dwellings are more susceptible to undetected damage and unlawful entry. Vacancy status must be evaluated from the known facts.

Document the vacancy status and the supporting facts. Record the date vacancy was reported or discovered.

**250.G** Refer any vacant dwelling with signs of vandalism, theft, or unlawful entry. Obtain underwriting direction before altering the vacancy classification.

Prior loss activity may indicate an unmanaged premises. Prompt review supports appropriate risk action.

Document observed or reported damage in the file. Retain inspection material when available.

**250.H** Require prompt notice of any change from owner occupancy to nonowner occupancy. Refer the risk when the change affects eligibility or rating.

Occupancy changes can materially alter the hazard. Timely notice permits accurate underwriting action.

Document the reported change and effective circumstances. Record all underwriting decisions.

**250.I** Distinguish vacancy from temporary absence based on the condition of the premises. Refer uncertain situations for review.

A furnished dwelling may still lack meaningful occupancy. Accurate classification depends on the total risk condition.

Document the facts supporting the classification. Record the source of all occupancy information.

**250.J** Confirm whether the dwelling remains furnished during an absence. Refer any premises stripped of customary household contents.

Removal of customary contents can indicate vacancy or abandonment. Reduced contents may also delay discovery of loss.

Document the observed or reported contents condition. Retain inspection notes or images when available.

**250.K** Confirm that heat, water, and electrical service are maintained when required for safe occupancy. Refer any lapse in essential service.

Utility interruption can increase freeze, fire, and deterioration exposure. Service status is material to vacancy evaluation.

Document the reported utility condition. Record corrective action when disclosed.

**250.L** Require ongoing maintenance of the dwelling and grounds. Refer any risk with apparent neglect or deferred repairs.

Poor maintenance increases the likelihood and severity of loss. Neglected grounds can also signal limited supervision.

Document visible maintenance concerns. Retain inspection findings and applicant explanations.

**250.M** Obtain an inspection when occupancy or vacancy facts cannot be verified through reliable information. Refer material discrepancies.

Inspection can confirm the actual condition and use of the premises. Conflicting information requires underwriting resolution.

Document the inspection request and result. Record how discrepancies were resolved.

**250.N** Confirm whether a caretaker has access to an unattended dwelling. Refer any arrangement lacking a responsible contact.

Caretaker access can support timely discovery of damage. Informal arrangements may not provide dependable oversight.

Document the caretaker arrangement and contact information. Record the scope of the caretaker's responsibilities.

**250.O** Confirm that mail, deliveries, and notices are monitored at an unattended premises. Refer any indication that communications are ignored.

Uncollected communications can indicate abandonment or limited supervision. Missed notices may also delay response to hazards.

Document the monitoring arrangement. Record the responsible contact in the file.

**250.P** Refer any dwelling undergoing construction, substantial repair, or rehabilitation. Do not treat active work as ordinary resident occupancy.

Active work can create fire, theft, water, and liability hazards. Contractor activity may also affect occupancy status.

Document the nature of the work. Record the parties responsible for site security.

**250.Q** Refer any premises where repairs prevent normal habitation. Confirm whether the dwelling is occupied, vacant, or held for future occupancy.

Uninhabitable conditions can change the exposure materially. Work activity may mask a vacancy condition.

Document the reported habitability condition. Retain available repair descriptions.

**250.R** Refer any dwelling listed for sale when occupancy is changing or uncertain. Confirm who will maintain the premises until transfer.

Sale activity can result in reduced supervision or delayed maintenance. Vacant sale properties require careful review.

Document the sale status and responsible contact. Record the stated occupancy condition.

**250.S** Refer any dwelling held by an estate, trust, or similar ownership arrangement when occupancy is unclear. Confirm authority to manage the risk.

Nontraditional ownership can complicate maintenance and claim authority. Clear management responsibility is required.

Document the ownership arrangement and authorized contact. Retain supporting information supplied for review.

**250.T** Refer any dwelling affected by separation, dissolution, or household displacement when occupancy is uncertain. Confirm who remains responsible for the premises.

Household changes can leave a dwelling unattended or inadequately maintained. Conflicting occupancy statements require resolution.

Document the reported household change. Record the responsible party for maintenance and access.

**250.U** Refer any dwelling occupied by a tenant. Confirm that the insured's role is accurately described.

Tenant occupancy presents landlord exposures not present in resident ownership. Proper classification depends on the insured's role.

Document the tenant occupancy status. Record the insured's stated responsibilities.

**250.V** Obtain lease information when tenant occupancy is material to the risk. Refer any occupancy arrangement that is informal, disputed, or unclear.

A written lease can clarify possession and maintenance duties. Unclear occupancy arrangements can complicate loss investigation.

Document the available lease details. Retain relevant lease material in the file.

**250.W** Refer any rental use not disclosed at application or renewal. Do not continue the risk without confirming the rental exposure.

Undisclosed rental activity can materially change occupancy and liability exposure. Accurate disclosure supports correct underwriting treatment.

Document the rental use reported or discovered. Record the underwriting disposition.

**250.X** Refer any request involving a short-term rental allowance. Confirm that the dwelling use remains within the approved underwriting appetite.

Short-term rental activity can increase turnover, liability, and property loss exposure. The arrangement requires clear review of actual use.

Document the request and occupancy details. Record the approval or declination rationale.

**250.Y** Apply the landlord contents sublimit only when the insured's contents are connected with the rental premises. Refer uncertain ownership of contents.

Landlord contents may have a different purpose and loss profile than household property. Clear ownership supports proper claim handling.

Document the contents described by the insured. Record the connection between the contents and rental use.

**250.Z** Confirm that tenants are selected and managed by the insured or an authorized representative. Refer any arrangement where occupancy control is absent.

Lack of occupancy control can increase property damage and liability exposure. Management responsibility must be identifiable.

Document the party responsible for tenant selection. Record the stated management practices.

**250.AA** Confirm that the insured retains lawful access to inspect and maintain a rental dwelling. Refer any arrangement that prevents reasonable oversight.

Access supports prompt discovery of damage and needed repairs. Restricted access can increase loss severity.

Document the access arrangement. Record any limitations disclosed by the insured.

**250.AB** Refer any rental dwelling where maintenance duties are assigned solely to an occupant. Confirm that the insured retains adequate oversight.

Occupant maintenance duties may not ensure proper protection of the premises. The owner remains exposed to property deterioration.

Document the stated maintenance responsibilities. Record the party expected to perform repairs.

**250.AC** Refer any home-sharing or platform-based rental activity. Confirm the extent and nature of guest use.

Guest turnover can create occupancy conditions unlike ordinary tenancy. Platform activity may involve undisclosed commercial characteristics.

Document the platform use described by the insured. Record the underwriting decision.

**250.AD** Refer any dwelling used to house employees, contractors, or work crews. Confirm who controls occupancy and maintenance.

Employer-related housing can create heightened liability and property exposure. Control of the premises must be clear.

Document the occupant relationship to the insured. Record the responsible maintenance contact.

**250.AE** Refer any dwelling used for rooming, boarding, or similar lodging activity. Do not classify lodging activity as ordinary household occupancy.

Lodging activity can increase guest turnover and liability exposure. The use may exceed personal lines appetite.

Document the lodging use and operator information. Record the referral outcome.

**250.AF** Refer any dwelling where occupants pay rent to someone other than the named insured. Confirm the insured's interest and authority.

Payment to another party may indicate an undisclosed ownership or management arrangement. The arrangement can affect eligibility.

Document the payee relationship described by the applicant. Record available ownership information.

**250.AG** Refer any dwelling occupied without a clear agreement for possession. Confirm whether the occupancy is permitted and stable.

Informal possession can create eviction, liability, and maintenance concerns. Lack of clarity may impair claim investigation.

Document the occupancy arrangement as reported. Record the basis for underwriting acceptance or referral.

**250.AH** Confirm whether any portion of the dwelling is rented while the insured remains in residence. Refer any mixed occupancy that is not clearly described.

Mixed occupancy can change property, liability, and eligibility considerations. The occupied areas and rental areas must be understood.

Document the use of the dwelling areas. Record the insured's stated residence status.

**250.AI** Refer any dwelling where a detached structure is occupied by a tenant, guest, or other nonhousehold occupant. Confirm the use of that structure.

Detached structure occupancy can create separate liability and property exposure. The use may not align with the dwelling classification.

Document the structure occupancy and intended use. Record the underwriting determination.

**250.AJ** Refer any premises where access is shared with unrelated occupants. Confirm responsibility for common areas and exterior maintenance.

Shared access can create uncertain control over hazardous conditions. Common area responsibility affects liability exposure.

Document the parties responsible for common areas. Record the stated maintenance arrangement.

**250.AK** Require disclosure of known prior vacancy, abandonment, or occupancy disputes affecting the premises. Refer material adverse history.

Prior conditions may reveal recurring supervision or maintenance concerns. Relevant history supports sound risk evaluation.

Document the history reported by the applicant. Record the source of any adverse information.

**250.AL** Refer any dwelling with signs that it is abandoned. Confirm whether the insured intends to maintain or dispose of the property.

Abandonment indicators can signal severe deterioration and delayed loss discovery. Immediate underwriting review is necessary.

Document the observed abandonment indicators. Retain inspection material when available.

**250.AM** Refer any premises with boarded openings, damaged locks, or impaired security. Confirm whether the condition is related to vacancy or repair activity.

Impaired security increases exposure to unlawful entry and vandalism. The cause and corrective plan must be known.

Document the security condition. Record corrective measures reported by the insured.

**250.AN** Confirm that vacant premises are protected from weather-related damage. Refer any condition suggesting inadequate property preservation.

Vacancy can allow minor damage to become severe before discovery. Property preservation supports loss prevention.

Document the protective measures reported. Record available inspection observations.

**250.AO** Refer any dwelling with prior water damage that remains vacant or lightly supervised. Confirm that repairs and mitigation are complete.

Prior water damage can recur or conceal deterioration. Vacancy can delay recognition of continuing moisture conditions.

Document the prior damage and repair status. Retain available repair evidence.

**250.AP** Refer any dwelling with prior fire damage that is not fully restored and occupied. Confirm current habitability and site security.

Fire damage can leave structural and electrical hazards. Incomplete restoration may materially increase loss exposure.

Document the fire damage condition. Record available repair and inspection information.

**250.AQ** Refer any dwelling where a tenant has departed and personal property remains. Confirm possession status before making occupancy assumptions.

Remaining property may indicate an unresolved tenancy or abandonment issue. The facts can affect access and loss handling.

Document the reported possession status. Record the source of the information.

**250.AR** Refer any dwelling subject to foreclosure, repossession, or similar transfer pressure. Confirm who has control of maintenance and access.

Financial distress can increase vacancy, neglect, and security exposure. Control of the premises may change without notice.

Document the reported transfer condition. Record the responsible contact for property care.

**250.AS** Refer any risk where the insured cannot be contacted regarding occupancy information. Do not rely on stale or incomplete statements.

Unavailable insureds can prevent confirmation of material risk facts. Current information is necessary for underwriting action.

Document all contact attempts. Record the basis for any disposition.

**250.AT** Confirm that application statements regarding occupancy are consistent with inspection findings. Refer all material inconsistencies.

Conflicting information can indicate misclassification or undisclosed use. Underwriting must resolve the actual exposure.

Document the inconsistency and the explanation received. Record the final classification.

**250.AU** Confirm that occupancy information remains current at renewal. Refer any reported or discovered change in dwelling use.

Renewal processing must reflect the risk as currently presented. Prior information may no longer be reliable.

Document the updated occupancy statement. Record any underwriting action taken.

**250.AV** Refer any dwelling used for business lodging, guest accommodation, or similar revenue activity. Confirm whether the use remains personal in nature.

Revenue activity can create exposures outside ordinary personal occupancy. The actual operations determine underwriting treatment.

Document the revenue activity described. Record the referral decision.

**250.AW** Refer any dwelling where the insured stores business property while the premises are vacant or rented. Confirm the nature of the stored property.

Business property can increase theft, fire, and liability exposure. Storage use may also alter the dwelling's classification.

Document the stored property description. Record the stated purpose of storage.

**250.AX** Refer any risk involving subleasing, assignment of tenancy, or unauthorized occupants. Confirm who has possession of the premises.

Unauthorized occupancy can impair maintenance, access, and claim investigation. Possession must be accurately established.

Document the reported occupancy arrangement. Record the source of verification.

**250.AY** Confirm that emergency contacts can access the premises when the insured is absent. Refer any risk lacking reliable emergency access.

Emergency access can limit damage from water, fire, or security events. Delayed entry may increase loss severity.

Document the emergency contact arrangement. Record contact information in the file.

**250.AZ** Refer any dwelling where keys, access codes, or security controls are broadly shared. Confirm who can enter the premises.

Uncontrolled access can increase theft and liability exposure. Security responsibility must be clearly assigned.
